### Question 24: Glucocorticoid Suppression of Hypothalamic ADH: Water Intoxication in Adrenal Insufficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Glucocorticoids (cortisol) exert tonic negative feedback on the hypothalamus to suppress ADH (vasopressin) secretion, preventing excessive water retention and hyponatremia, especially during water loading. (糖皮质激素通过负反馈抑制下丘脑 ADH 分泌，防止过度保水和低钠血症，尤其是在水负荷时。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 36-year-old woman with a history of fatigue, weight loss, and hyperpigmentation presents to the emergency department after becoming obtunded, nauseated, and experiencing a generalized tonic-clonic seizure. She reports drinking 1.5 liters of tap water over one hour earlier in the day. Her vital signs show a blood pressure of 85/50 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, and respiratory rate of 20 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals dry mucous membranes and poor skin turgor. Laboratory results show serum sodium of 114 mEq/L (normal 135-145 mEq/L), serum potassium of 5.8 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), serum osmolality of 238 mOsm/kg (normal 275-295 mOsm/kg), plasma BUN of 18 mg/dL (normal 7-20 mg/dL), and plasma creatinine of 0.8 mg/dL (normal 0.6-1.2 mg/dL). Urine studies reveal a urine osmolality of 480 mOsm/kg (normal 50-1200 mOsm/kg, but inappropriately high given the low serum osmolality) and a urine sodium concentration of 60 mEq/L (normal 20-120 mEq/L). A random plasma cortisol level is 2 mcg/dL (normal 5-25 mcg/dL). Given this clinical presentation and laboratory findings, which physiological hormone is critically required to tonically restrain hypothalamic ADH release and permit the normal renal excretion of a free water load?

(A) Aldosterone
(B) Atrial Natriuretic Peptide (ANP)
(C) Cortisol
(D) Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2)
(E) Thyrotropin-Releasing Hormone (TRH)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 36-year-old woman with symptoms suggestive of adrenal insufficiency (fatigue, weight loss, hyperpigmentation) presenting with acute neurological symptoms (obtundation, seizure) after drinking water. This points towards hyponatremia.
- **Vital Signs:** Hypotension (BP 85/50 mmHg) and tachycardia (HR 110 bpm) are consistent with volume depletion and/or adrenal crisis due to cortisol deficiency.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Severe hyponatremia (Na 114 mEq/L) and low serum osmolality (238 mOsm/kg) confirm hypotonic hyponatremia.
    - Elevated urine osmolality (480 mOsm/kg) despite low serum osmolality indicates impaired water excretion (inappropriately concentrated urine).
    - Elevated urine sodium (60 mEq/L) suggests volume depletion and/or mineralocorticoid deficiency (aldosterone), but also points away from typical SIADH where urine sodium is usually >40 mEq/L but often higher.
    - Low random cortisol (2 mcg/dL) confirms adrenal insufficiency.
    - Hyperkalemia (K 5.8 mEq/L) is consistent with aldosterone deficiency in primary adrenal insufficiency.
- **Water Load Challenge:** The patient developed severe symptoms after drinking 1.5L water, indicating an inability to excrete the free water load.
- **Synthesis:** The patient has primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease), leading to both cortisol and aldosterone deficiency. The cortisol deficiency impairs the normal suppression of ADH, leading to water retention. The aldosterone deficiency contributes to sodium loss and volume depletion, further stimulating ADH. The combination results in severe hyponatremia and inability to excrete a water load. The question asks which hormone is required to restrain hypothalamic ADH release.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **ADH Regulation:** ADH (vasopressin) secretion from the posterior pituitary is regulated by both osmotic and non-osmotic stimuli.
    - **Osmotic Regulation:** Decreased plasma osmolality (detected by circumventricular organ osmoreceptors, primarily in the OVLT) inhibits ADH secretion. Increased plasma osmolality stimulates ADH secretion.
    - **Non-Osmotic Regulation:** Decreased effective circulating volume (detected by baroreceptors in the carotid sinus, aortic arch, and atria) stimulates ADH secretion. Nausea, pain, stress, and certain drugs also stimulate ADH.
- **Cortisol's Role:** Glucocorticoids (cortisol) play a crucial role in regulating ADH secretion, primarily through tonic negative feedback on the hypothalamus.
    - Cortisol crosses the blood-brain barrier and acts on hypothalamic neurons (both parvocellular CRH neurons and magnocellular ADH neurons).
    - Cortisol suppresses the transcription and release of both CRH and ADH.
    - In cortisol deficiency (e.g., Addison disease), this negative feedback is lost, leading to increased basal ADH secretion.
    - Furthermore, cortisol deficiency causes hypotension, which provides a potent non-osmotic stimulus for ADH release via baroreceptor activation.
    - The combination of loss of tonic inhibition and increased non-osmotic stimulation results in inappropriately high ADH levels in adrenal insufficiency.
- **ADH Action on Kidney:** ADH binds to V2 receptors on the basolateral membrane of principal cells in the collecting duct. This activates adenylyl cyclase, increasing intracellular cAMP, which activates protein kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels, causing them to translocate from intracellular vesicles to the apical membrane, increasing water permeability and allowing water reabsorption.
- **Water Intoxication:** In this patient, the high ADH levels (due to cortisol deficiency and volume depletion) prevent the excretion of the ingested free water load. This leads to worsening hyponatremia, water shifting into brain cells causing cerebral edema, and neurological symptoms (obtundation, seizure).
- **Other Hormones:**
    - Aldosterone: Primarily regulates sodium and potassium balance, affecting volume status which indirectly influences ADH, but does not directly restrain hypothalamic ADH release.
    - ANP: Released in response to atrial stretch, promotes natriuresis and diuresis, inhibiting ADH and renin release. It does not restrain hypothalamic ADH.
    - PGE2: Can inhibit ADH release and promote water excretion, but is not the primary tonic regulator.
    - TRH: Stimulates TSH and prolactin release; has a permissive effect on ADH release but does not restrain it.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with symptoms of adrenal insufficiency (fatigue, weight loss, hyperpigmentation) and acute neurological symptoms after water intake.
2. Labs confirm severe hypotonic hyponatremia (low Na, low serum Osm) with inappropriately concentrated urine (high urine Osm) and low cortisol.
3. The inability to excrete a water load despite low serum osmolality indicates excessive ADH activity.
4. Adrenal insufficiency leads to both cortisol and aldosterone deficiency.
5. Aldosterone deficiency contributes to sodium loss and volume depletion, stimulating ADH via baroreceptors.
6. Cortisol deficiency removes the tonic negative feedback inhibition on hypothalamic ADH release.
7. The combination of loss of inhibition and increased stimulation results in high ADH, preventing water excretion and causing water intoxication.
8. The question asks for the hormone that tonically restrains hypothalamic ADH release. Cortisol fits this description.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Aldosterone:** Aldosterone regulates sodium reabsorption in the distal nephron and collecting duct, influencing volume status. While aldosterone deficiency contributes to volume depletion, which stimulates ADH, aldosterone itself does not directly restrain hypothalamic ADH release. Its primary role is mineralocorticoid. Incorrect.
- **(B) Atrial Natriuretic Peptide (ANP):** ANP is released in response to atrial stretch (volume expansion) and promotes sodium and water excretion, inhibiting ADH and renin. It opposes ADH action and does not restrain its hypothalamic release. Incorrect.
- **(C) Cortisol:** Cortisol exerts tonic negative feedback on the hypothalamus, suppressing both CRH and ADH secretion. Deficiency leads to increased ADH. This is the correct answer. Correct.
- **(D) Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2):** PGE2 can inhibit ADH release and promote water excretion, particularly in the kidney. However, it is not the primary tonic regulator of hypothalamic ADH release. Its role is more modulatory. Incorrect.
- **(E) Thyrotropin-Releasing Hormone (TRH):** TRH primarily stimulates TSH and prolactin release from the anterior pituitary. It can stimulate ADH release, especially in conditions like primary polydipsia or during stress, but it does not restrain hypothalamic ADH release. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Hormone        | Primary Function                                  | Effect on ADH Release (Hypothalamus) | Effect on Water Excretion (Kidney) | Relevance to Case |
|----------------|---------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------|-------------------|
| Cortisol       | Glucocorticoid; stress response, metabolism      | Tonic Inhibition                     | Indirect (via ADH)                 | Key: Deficiency leads to ↑ADH |
| Aldosterone    | Mineralocorticoid; Na+ reabsorption, K+ secretion | Indirect (via volume)                | Indirect (via Na+ reabsorption)    | Contributes to volume depletion ↑ADH |
| ANP            | Natriuresis, diuresis (volume expansion)        | Inhibition                           | Inhibition                        | Opposite effect |
| PGE2           | Local mediator; inflammation, renal function     | Inhibition                           | Promotion                         | Modulatory |
| TRH            | Stimulates TSH/Prolactin                         | Stimulation                          | Stimulation                       | Incorrect |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Glucocorticoids (cortisol) exert critical tonic negative feedback on the hypothalamus, suppressing ADH secretion. Deficiency states, like Addison disease, remove this inhibition, leading to inappropriately high ADH levels, impaired free water excretion, and increased susceptibility to hyponatremia and water intoxication, especially when challenged with a water load. Understanding this regulatory mechanism is crucial for diagnosing and managing endocrine disorders affecting fluid balance.

---

### Question 25: Thyroid Hormone and Renal Water Excretion: Hyponatremia in Severe Hypothyroidism
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Severe primary hypothyroidism leads to decreased cardiac output, resulting in arterial underfilling, which non-osmotically stimulates ADH secretion via baroreceptor activation, impairing free water excretion and causing euvolemic hyponatremia. (严重原发性甲状腺功能减退症导致心输出量下降，引起动脉灌注不足，通过条索体感受器激活非渗透性ADH分泌，损害游离水排泄，引起等容性低钠血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a history of long-standing, untreated Hashimoto thyroiditis presents to the emergency department with worsening lethargy, periorbital puffiness, and delayed deep tendon reflexes. Her thyroid function tests show a markedly elevated TSH of 88 mIU/L (normal range: 0.4-4.0 mIU/L) and a very low free T4 level of 0.2 ng/dL (normal range: 0.8-1.8 ng/dL). Vital signs reveal a heart rate of 55 bpm and blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg. Physical examination shows no signs of dehydration, edema, or ascites. Laboratory results include serum sodium of 122 mEq/L (normal range: 135-145 mEq/L), serum osmolality of 252 mOsm/kg (normal range: 275-295 mOsm/kg), plasma vasopressin level of 15 pg/mL (normal range: 1-5 pg/mL), and urine osmolality of 410 mOsm/kg (normal range: 50-1200 mOsm/kg). The patient is clinically euvolemic. Which of the following hemodynamic mechanisms is primarily responsible for the elevated plasma vasopressin level in this patient?

(A) Decreased renal blood flow leading to increased renin secretion and angiotensin II-mediated ADH release.
(B) Increased atrial stretch due to volume overload stimulating the Gauer-Henry reflex and inhibiting ADH release.
(C) Decreased cardiac output causing arterial underfilling and baroreceptor activation, leading to non-osmotic ADH secretion.
(D) Increased central nervous system osmolality due to impaired water excretion stimulating central osmoreceptors and ADH release.
(E) Decreased GFR leading to reduced urea excretion and increased medullary hypertonicity, enhancing ADH's effect on the collecting duct.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman with untreated Hashimoto thyroiditis (severe hypothyroidism).
- **Symptoms:** Lethargy, periorbital puffiness, delayed reflexes (classic signs of hypothyroidism).
- **Labs:**
    - TSH 88 mIU/L, free T4 0.2 ng/dL: Confirms severe primary hypothyroidism.
    - Serum Na 122 mEq/L, Serum Osm 252 mOsm/kg: Hyponatremia and hypo-osmolality.
    - Urine Osm 410 mOsm/kg: Inappropriately concentrated urine given the low serum osmolality, suggesting ADH effect.
    - Plasma Vasopressin 15 pg/mL: Elevated ADH level.
- **Physical Exam:** Euvolemic (no signs of dehydration or overload).
- **Diagnosis:** Euvolemic hyponatremia secondary to severe hypothyroidism. The question asks for the *primary hemodynamic mechanism* stimulating ADH.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Hypothyroidism and Cardiac Function:** Severe hypothyroidism reduces myocardial contractility, decreases heart rate (bradycardia), and impairs diastolic relaxation, leading to a significant reduction in cardiac output (CO).
- **Arterial Underfilling:** Reduced CO results in decreased effective circulating arterial volume (arterial underfilling). This means the pressure sensed by the baroreceptors in the carotid sinus and aortic arch is lower than normal, despite the patient being clinically euvolemic (total body water is normal, but the effective arterial volume is low).
- **Baroreceptor Reflex:** The carotid sinus and aortic arch baroreceptors are sensitive to changes in arterial pressure and volume. Arterial underfilling triggers these baroreceptors to increase their firing rate.
- **ADH Secretion:** Afferent signals from the baroreceptors travel via the glossopharyngeal (CN IX) and vagus (CN X) nerves to the nucleus tractus solitarius (NTS) in the brainstem. The NTS projects to the supraoptic nucleus (SON) and paraventricular nucleus (PVN) of the hypothalamus, which contain magnocellular neurons that synthesize vasopressin (ADH). The increased baroreceptor activity stimulates these hypothalamic neurons to synthesize and release ADH from the posterior pituitary. This is a non-osmotic stimulus for ADH secretion.
- **Renal Effects of ADH:** ADH acts on the V2 receptors in the principal cells of the collecting duct. Activation of V2 receptors stimulates adenylyl cyclase, increasing intracellular cAMP levels. cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA), which phosphorylates aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels. Phosphorylated AQP2 channels translocate from intracellular vesicles to the apical membrane of the principal cells, increasing water permeability. This allows water to be reabsorbed from the tubular fluid into the hypertonic medullary interstitium, concentrating the urine and diluting the plasma, leading to hyponatremia.
- **Other Factors:** While decreased GFR (due to reduced renal blood flow) contributes to impaired free water excretion by reducing the amount of fluid delivered to the diluting segments, the *primary stimulus* for ADH secretion in this context is the hemodynamic change (decreased CO leading to arterial underfilling). Central osmoreceptors are not the primary driver here, as serum osmolality is low.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has severe hypothyroidism confirmed by lab values (high TSH, low free T4).
2. The patient presents with euvolemic hyponatremia (low serum Na, low serum Osm, inappropriately high urine Osm, euvolemic exam).
3. The plasma vasopressin level is elevated, indicating ADH is the cause of the concentrated urine and hyponatremia.
4. The question asks for the *primary hemodynamic mechanism* stimulating ADH.
5. Severe hypothyroidism impairs cardiac function, leading to decreased cardiac output.
6. Decreased cardiac output causes arterial underfilling.
7. Arterial underfilling activates baroreceptors.
8. Baroreceptor activation stimulates non-osmotic ADH release.
9. Therefore, decreased cardiac output causing arterial underfilling and baroreceptor activation is the primary hemodynamic mechanism.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Decreased renal blood flow leading to increased renin secretion and angiotensin II-mediated ADH release.** While decreased renal blood flow occurs in hypothyroidism and can stimulate renin release, the primary stimulus for ADH in this specific scenario is arterial underfilling sensed by systemic baroreceptors, not renal baroreceptors or the renin-angiotensin system. The renin-angiotensin system primarily regulates sodium and water balance through aldosterone and thirst, not directly through ADH in response to low CO.
- **(B) Increased atrial stretch due to volume overload stimulating the Gauer-Henry reflex and inhibiting ADH release.** This describes the opposite situation. Atrial stretch (volume overload) stimulates the Gauer-Henry reflex, which *inhibits* ADH release. This patient is euvolemic, not volume overloaded.
- **(C) Decreased cardiac output causing arterial underfilling and baroreceptor activation, leading to non-osmotic ADH secretion.** This accurately describes the pathophysiology. Severe hypothyroidism impairs cardiac function, leading to reduced CO and arterial underfilling, which activates systemic baroreceptors, causing non-osmotic ADH release. This is the correct mechanism.
- **(D) Increased central nervous system osmolality due to impaired water excretion stimulating central osmoreceptors and ADH release.** Central osmoreceptors are stimulated by *increased* plasma osmolality (hyperosmolality), not decreased osmolality. This patient has hypo-osmolality (serum Osm 252 mOsm/kg). Therefore, central osmoreceptors are suppressed, not stimulated.
- **(E) Decreased GFR leading to reduced urea excretion and increased medullary hypertonicity, enhancing ADH's effect on the collecting duct.** Decreased GFR does occur in hypothyroidism and contributes to impaired free water excretion by reducing distal delivery. Increased medullary hypertonicity enhances the *effect* of ADH on water reabsorption, but it does not *stimulate* ADH secretion. The primary stimulus for secretion is the hemodynamic change described in option C.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Hypothyroidism-Induced Hyponatremia | SIADH                       | Primary Polydipsia            | Nephrogenic DI              |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Primary Cause**        | Decreased CO -> Baroreceptor ADH     | Ectopic ADH/CNS/Drugs       | Excessive water intake       | Renal resistance to ADH     |
| **Volume Status**        | Euvolemic                           | Euvolemic                   | Euvolemic                     | Variable (often dehydrated) |
| **Serum Na**             | Low                                 | Low                         | Low (if intake > excretion)  | Variable (often high)       |
| **Serum Osm**            | Low                                 | Low                         | Low                           | Variable                   |
| **Urine Osm**            | High (inappropriate)                | High (inappropriate)        | Low (dilute)                  | Low (dilute)                |
| **Plasma ADH**           | High                                | High                        | Low (suppressed)             | High (compensatory)         |
| **GFR**                  | Low                                 | Normal                      | Normal                        | Normal                     |
| **Response to dDAVP**    | N/A                                 | N/A                         | Urine concentrates (>600)    | No change                  |
| **Response to Fluid Restriction** | Resolves                          | Resolves                    | Resolves                      | N/A                        |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Severe primary hypothyroidism impairs cardiac function, leading to decreased cardiac output and subsequent arterial underfilling. This hemodynamic change activates systemic baroreceptors, triggering non-osmotic release of ADH. The elevated ADH levels, combined with reduced distal nephron fluid delivery due to decreased GFR, impair free water excretion, resulting in euvolemic dilutional hyponatremia. This condition typically resolves with thyroid hormone replacement therapy.

---

### Question 26: [Posterior Pituitary & Fluid Balance Mechanism / Neuroendocrine Reflexes]
- **Difficulty**: [Medium]
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: [The physiological mechanism of the milk ejection reflex (letdown reflex), involving the neuroendocrine pathway from nipple stimulation to oxytocin release and subsequent contraction of specific mammary gland cells. 催乳反射的神经内分泌机制：乳头刺激到催产素释放，进而引起乳腺特定细胞收缩。]

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old primiparous woman, 4 weeks postpartum, is breastfeeding her infant. She reports that when her baby begins suckling, she experiences a sensation of fullness and tingling in her breasts, followed by milk spraying from the nipple. She notes that this reflex occurs reliably within seconds of the baby latching on. She also mentions that sometimes, simply hearing her baby cry from another room can trigger the same sensation and milk ejection, even without the baby physically feeding. She denies any history of breast surgery, hormonal contraception use, or significant medical conditions. Her infant is thriving and gaining weight appropriately. She is otherwise healthy and well-hydrated.

Which of the following cell types is directly responsible for the forceful expulsion of milk from the mammary alveoli during the milk ejection reflex?

(A) Alveolar epithelial cells
(B) Fibroblasts in the mammary stroma
(C) Myoepithelial cells
(D) Endothelial cells lining the lactiferous ducts
(E) Adipocytes in the surrounding breast tissue

#### Correct Answer: [C]

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old primiparous woman, 4 weeks postpartum, breastfeeding. This sets the context for lactation physiology.
- **Chief Complaint/Observation:** Milk ejection reflex triggered by suckling (physical stimulus) and hearing baby cry (conditioned stimulus). This points towards a neuroendocrine reflex involving oxytocin.
- **Mechanism Description:** Sensation of fullness/tingling followed by milk spraying. This describes the physical manifestation of myoepithelial cell contraction.
- **Timing:** Reflex occurs within seconds, indicating a rapid neurohormonal response.
- **Key Physiological Process:** The question asks for the specific cells responsible for milk expulsion. This requires knowledge of mammary gland anatomy and the hormonal control of lactation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Afferent Pathway:** Suckling stimulates mechanoreceptors in the nipple/areola. Sensory signals travel via spinal nerves (T4-T6) to the hypothalamus, specifically the paraventricular nucleus (PVN).
- **Central Processing:** The PVN contains magnocellular neurons that synthesize oxytocin.
- **Efferent Pathway:** Stimulation of PVN neurons triggers action potentials that travel down the axons of these neurons to the posterior pituitary (neurohypophysis).
- **Hormone Release:** Action potentials cause the exocytosis of oxytocin-containing vesicles (neurosecretory granules) into the bloodstream.
- **Target Tissue:** Oxytocin travels via the circulation to the mammary glands.
- **Receptor Binding:** Oxytocin binds to Gq-coupled oxytocin receptors (OTR) located on the plasma membrane of myoepithelial cells surrounding the mammary alveoli and ducts.
- **Signal Transduction:** OTR activation leads to activation of phospholipase C (PLC), generating inositol trisphosphate (IP3) and diacylglycerol (DAG). IP3 causes release of Ca2+ from intracellular stores, and DAG activates protein kinase C (PKC). Increased intracellular Ca2+ is the primary trigger for contraction.
- **Cellular Response:** The increased intracellular Ca2+ concentration causes the myoepithelial cells to contract.
- **Milk Ejection:** Contraction of these cells squeezes the alveoli and ducts, forcing milk towards the nipple.
- **Prolactin vs. Oxytocin:** Prolactin (from anterior pituitary) stimulates milk *synthesis* by alveolar epithelial cells. Oxytocin stimulates milk *ejection* by myoepithelial cells.
- **Conditioned Reflex:** Higher brain centers can also stimulate the PVN, leading to oxytocin release and milk ejection in response to non-physical stimuli like a baby's cry (Pavlovian conditioning).

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient describes the milk ejection reflex (letdown reflex).
2. This reflex is known to be mediated by the hormone oxytocin released from the posterior pituitary.
3. Oxytocin acts on specific cells within the mammary gland to cause milk expulsion.
4. The question asks to identify these specific cells.
5. Recall the roles of different cell types in the mammary gland: Alveolar epithelial cells produce milk (stimulated by prolactin). Myoepithelial cells surround the alveoli and ducts and contract to expel milk (stimulated by oxytocin).
6. Therefore, myoepithelial cells are the target cells for oxytocin in the milk ejection reflex.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Alveolar epithelial cells:** These cells are responsible for synthesizing milk components (proteins, lipids, lactose) under the stimulation of prolactin. They do not contract to eject milk. Incorrect.
- **(B) Fibroblasts in the mammary stroma:** Fibroblasts are connective tissue cells providing structural support. They are not directly involved in the milk ejection mechanism. Incorrect.
- **(C) Myoepithelial cells:** These are specialized contractile cells located around the alveoli and ducts. They receive innervation and respond to oxytocin by contracting, thereby squeezing milk out. This is the correct answer. Correct.
- **(D) Endothelial cells lining the lactiferous ducts:** Endothelial cells form the lining of blood vessels and ducts. While ducts are involved in milk transport, the contraction responsible for ejection comes from the surrounding myoepithelial cells, not the endothelial lining itself. Incorrect.
- **(E) Adipocytes in the surrounding breast tissue:** Adipocytes are fat cells providing cushioning and energy storage. They play no direct role in the milk ejection reflex. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | Prolactin (Anterior Pituitary) | Oxytocin (Posterior Pituitary) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| **Primary Action**   | Milk Synthesis (Production)     | Milk Ejection (Letdown)        |
| **Target Cells**     | Alveolar Epithelial Cells       | Myoepithelial Cells            |
| **Stimulus for Release** | Suckling, Stress, Sleep        | Suckling, Cervical/Uterine Stretch, Auditory Stimuli (Conditioned) |
| **Receptor Type**    | Prolactin Receptor (Jak-STAT)   | Oxytocin Receptor (Gq)         |
| **Cellular Effect**  | Increased Milk Production      | Myoepithelial Cell Contraction |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The milk ejection reflex is a neuroendocrine reflex initiated by nipple stimulation, leading to oxytocin release from the posterior pituitary. Oxytocin acts on myoepithelial cells surrounding the mammary alveoli, causing their contraction and forceful expulsion of milk. This distinguishes oxytocin's role in ejection from prolactin's role in production.
